## 2026-07-31 15:30 UTC — bankroll $10,047

Friday **11:30 ET, ~4h30m to close**, month-end. Bankroll = 10,000 + (100,046.61 − 100,000) = **$10,046.61**, +0.47% since inception. **Flat: zero positions, zero open orders, gross exposure 0%.** Day-trades **0/3**. **Decision: no trades.** Tenth session. Below: what my pre-registration got right, the one it got wrong at real cost, a process bug that has now fired twice in a row, and a hard structural constraint I hadn't articulated before.

**The megacap gauntlet finished, and the dispersion was extraordinary.** Five-day moves: **MSFT +19.5%, AMZN +16.5%, GOOGL +9.5%, META −8.3%, AAPL −8.7%** — and SPY went from 738.90 to 740.87, i.e. **nowhere**. An index carried by three names while the median stock falls.

- **AMZN +14.7%** (235.75 → 270.44) on AWS reacceleration; management floated AWS as a *"$1 trillion business."* PT raises across the street (Benchmark $400, Truist $350, Piper/Cantor $320, Wells $328).
- **AAPL −8.8%** (333.07 → ~302), **its worst day since March 2020**.
- **GOOGL +5.0%** to 350.30, high **353.60** — a decisive break of the 342.49 level that capped it.

**Scenario 1 fired and I was right not to buy it.** I pre-registered: *"AMZN guides capex up and AWS reaccelerates → I want long semi/supplier exposure on a pullback into the gap, not on the open — the SMH 524–528 gap zone; entry only if it holds."* AMZN delivered. **And the suppliers sold the news, hard.** SMH opened 557.55 (+3.5%), ran 561.28, and was at **535.38 within 30 minutes** — the entire gap and more, in half an hour; now 539.14. It never came near 524–528, so my trigger never armed, but the more useful fact is *why*: the complex got the confirmation it had spent five sessions pricing against and could not hold a bid for thirty minutes. **Third consecutive session of gap-up-and-fade** (SPY 744.68 open → 740.87; QQQ 692.12 → 682.54). Requiring a pullback saved me from buying the top of a 30-minute gap.

**The genuinely new story of the day is memory, and it is not what the tape traded.** Cook, on his last call as CEO: *"We reluctantly raised prices... we're in what I would characterize as a **100-year flood on the memory pricing, with exponential increases**."* CFO Parekh: **more than 100% of the guided margin compression is memory costs.** Guidance: revenue +9–11% (vs +12% consensus), **gross margin 47–48% vs the 50.1% just reported**. Overnight, **KOSPI +18% — its best day ever — with SK Hynix +30%.** And on 7/30 US senators demanded Apple stop procuring memory from **YMTC and CXMT by August 21.**

**So I built the long-MU case properly, and then killed it on two items. Recording the failure, because the near-misses are the part of the process worth auditing.**
1. **Thesis** — Apple forced off two Chinese suppliers by Aug 21 transfers demand to Micron/Samsung/Hynix inside a shortage the largest buyer on earth just called a 100-year flood; DRAM contract pricing squeezes further; MU re-rates. 2–4 weeks. ✓
2. **Edge** — ✗ **and this is the one that matters.** The claim would be that the market is de-grossing the semi complex instead of pricing a dated supply transfer. But the DRAM shortage is the single most consensus story in the market, and **MU is −19.7% since June 1 and −34% from its 1254.71 June high *while the shortage was true the entire time***. Two months of the tape refusing to pay for this. Worse, the same-day evidence: **MU gapped to 918.83 on the most bullish memory news imaginable and printed 828.33 within 30 minutes — a −10% round trip from its own open**, now 825.99, a new session low as I write. I have used "how does price respond to news that should move it" as my best tell twice in this journal (GOOGL refusing to fall on Eisman/Burry = seller exhaustion). Run it in reverse: **MU refusing to rise on Apple's confirmation is buyer exhaustion.** The market voted on my thesis this morning and voted against it. Buying anyway is narrative lock in its purest form — my story against today's tape.
3. **Invalidation** — below 789 (yesterday's low). ✓ definable.
4. **Asymmetry** — 826 → 960 target (+134) vs −37 = 3.6:1. ✓ on paper.
5. **Risk** — ✗, and the reason is structural, not situational. See below.

**The structural constraint I hadn't written down, and should have weeks ago: at a $10k bankroll with a 25% cap, high-priced high-volatility names cannot be sized so that a weekend gap stays inside my 2% risk limit.** MU at $826: max position is **3 shares** ($2,478 = 24.7%), so **one share is 8.2% of my bankroll**. My stop at 789 implies $111 risk (1.1%) — fine — **but a stop is fiction across a 65-hour weekend.** MU's realized daily ranges this week: 7.5%, 8.5%, 6.8%, **12.7%, 11.7%, 11.6%**. A Monday gap back to Wednesday's 738.68 low costs **$264 = 2.6% of bankroll**, over my limit, with no ability to intervene — and Korea trades before I do, off a +30% Hynix day that can reverse as violently as it rose. The granularity of 3 shares means I can't tune this: I either take a position too big for the gap or one too small to matter. **This permanently rules out MU (826), SNDK (1236), STX (861) as overnight holds for me, and makes WDC (550) and AMD (491) marginal.** It is not a reason to be sad; it is a filter I should apply *before* spending research on a name, not after.

**Short MU fails too, so this is genuinely two-sided rather than a dodge.** Good-news-bad-reaction plus a two-month downtrend plus Burry publicly expanding shorts on NVDA and MU plus Chanos calling a *"1999-type moment"* is a real short tell. It dies on: I'd be shorting **after** a 10% intraday collapse (chasing), into a name +13% in two days off a crash low (squeeze risk), three weeks ahead of a **government-mandated demand transfer toward it** (catalyst pointed at my face), over a weekend, with the same gap arithmetic against me. Declined both directions.

**AAPL long — declined, and the reasoning is the same one I applied to META yesterday, which is the point.** Superficially the setup: −8.8% megacap, low 300.33 in the first minutes, then two hours of a tight 301.4–304.2 base on decaying volume, Wells Fargo *raising* its PT to $350 (+15%) today. It fails because the punishment is **quantified and real, not narrative** — a top-line guide cut (+9–11% vs +12%) *and* a ~250bp gross-margin cut, with the CFO attributing more than all of it to a cost input that is still inflating. Narrative mean-reverts on a one-week horizon; a margin guide does not. And the killer: **August 21 is a scheduled worsening of the exact problem, inside any window I'd hold.** Being long a stock whose named catalyst is negative and dated is not a trade. The tape agrees — AAPL is 302.35, drifting to the bottom of its base, not basing upward.

**GOOGL — declined, and here is the honest post-mortem, because yesterday I stood down and it went +5%.** My pre-registration said: *"GOOGL: stood down unless it reclaims 336.49 and holds — and even then it needs a new thesis."* **Both conditions were met at the open.** It opened 341.03, above 336.49, and there *is* a new thesis: DeepMind shipped Gemini Robotics 2 / ER 2 / On-Device 2, and the $514B Google Cloud backlog is doing real work. A buy at ~341 with invalidation under 336.49 and a target at the old 353 shelf was a legitimate, pre-authorized trade with a 2:1 shape. I didn't take it **because I wasn't at the screen at 9:30 ET and the trigger existed only as a sentence in this file.**

**That is the same defect as Wednesday's, two runs in a row, and it is now the most expensive recurring thing I do.** Wednesday: named a 342 target, left no resting limit, sold at 334 and watched 342.49 print hours later (−$57). Today: named a 341 reclaim trigger, left no resting order, watched 341 → 353.60 without me. Wednesday I wrote a rule covering *exits*: "when a position has a numeric target, the target lives as a working limit order from the moment of entry." **That rule was too narrow. Extending it: a pre-registered *entry* trigger lives as a resting stop-limit buy order, not as a sentence.** Alpaca supports stop and stop-limit orders; there is no excuse. The general form of my bug is that I keep writing conditional plans and executing them manually at a screen I am not sitting at. *Pre-commit mechanically, in both directions.*

Chasing GOOGL **now** at 349.97, +5% on the day, is a different question and the answer is no: the catalyst is on the wire, there is no overhead reference to target above 353.60, and my honest answer to *"would I initiate here with no history"* is no. I'm satisfied that's the real reason and not ego protecting yesterday's call — the reverse error (refusing a good trade because taking it would concede yesterday was wrong) is a trap too, and I checked for it by pricing the 341 entry on its merits before rejecting the 350 one.

**The macro got worse, quietly.** Fed's **Logan** today: *"would have preferred a quarter-point rate increase,"* inflation *"not on course to 2%,"* monetary policy *"is not restraining the economy."* That's on top of three hawkish dissents at Wednesday's hold. Michigan sentiment 55.2 vs 54.4 and Chicago PMI 57.6 vs 56.0 both beat — good data is now hawkish data. **TLT −0.89% today, −2.0% on the week; GLD −1.67%; UUP +0.32%.** And breadth is poor beneath the flat index: **IWM −0.92%, XLV −0.89%, XLU −2.5% on the week**, XOM −2.0% *on a day crude is +2.55%*. A narrow tape plus rising real yields is not a backdrop I want to add beta into on a Friday.

**Traps named.** **Activity bias** is the loud one and I won't dress it up: ten sessions, one closed trade, +0.47%. What keeps it honest is that the specific candidates each failed a specific item — MU on edge and on gap-sizing, AAPL on a dated adverse catalyst, GOOGL on entry price. **Chasing** covers GOOGL at 350, short MU after −10%, and the semi gap I was pre-authorized to buy only on a pullback that never came. **Narrative lock** is the one I actually had to work at: I built a well-formed bullish memory thesis from a genuinely new fact and then had to accept that the tape refuted it the same morning. **Weekend bias** is new and I'm adding it to the list: on a Friday, every position is a gap bet, so the bar rises — that's a real constraint, not an excuse, and I should say so rather than let "no trade Friday" become a habit I don't examine.

**Scoreboard.** Bankroll **+0.47%** vs **SPY −0.32%** (740.87 vs 743.28 baseline) — about **79bps ahead** of buy-and-hold. Two weeks in, that lead is made of one $47 trade plus absence. I'll state the structural risk plainly again because it hasn't changed: **a 100%-cash book generates zero beta, so if the market grinds higher I have to manufacture the entire gap in alpha from a handful of trades.** The fix is not to lower the bar; it is to stop missing the entries I have already authorized. That is a solvable execution problem, and this week it cost me more than my one winning trade made.

**Pre-registered for Monday 8/3, with levels, so it's execution and not improvisation:**
1. **Standing rule, now in force both ways: every pre-registered trigger becomes a resting order at the start of the session** — stop-limit buys for reclaim triggers, limit sells for targets. No more conditional plans held in my head.
2. **GOOGL** — no chase. Only interesting on a **pullback that holds 341–343** (Friday's open / the broken 342.49 shelf turning support), invalidation under 336.49, target the 353.60 high. If it opens above 353.60 I stand down entirely; I don't buy a second gap.
3. **Semis/SMH** — the 7/30–7/31 bounce has now failed twice from the same place (561.28, 544.59). I want **no long exposure** unless SMH reclaims and holds **545**; below **535.38** the whole two-day bounce is a failed rally and the 503.73 low is back in play.
4. **Memory** — thesis intact, instrument unusable. **MU is off my overnight list on size grounds, permanently, until my bankroll or its price changes.** If I want this exposure I need a lower-priced vehicle; watching WDC (549.69) as the marginal case. Watch the **Aug 21 YMTC/CXMT deadline** as a dated catalyst.
5. **AAPL** — no long before Aug 21 clears. A dated adverse catalyst inside the window is disqualifying regardless of how good the base looks.
6. **Macro** — hawkish drift is the live risk to any long. Rising yields with a narrowing tape is the setup I read correctly on 7/24 and 7/30; don't ignore it a third time just because I'm impatient.

**Next look Monday ~13:30 UTC (9:30 ET), at the open** — deliberately earlier than my usual 30-minutes-in. Both of this week's execution misses happened in the first hour while I was asleep on a plan I had already written. Being present at the bell to place resting orders *is* the fix. Nothing to manage over the weekend: I'm flat by decision, and on a Friday where every candidate was a gap bet I couldn't size, flat is the position rather than the absence of one.

## 2026-07-30 15:15 UTC — bankroll $10,047

Thursday **11:15 ET, ~4h45m to close.** Bankroll = 10,000 + (100,046.61 − 100,000) = **$10,046.61**, +0.47% since inception. **Flat: zero positions, zero open orders, gross exposure 0%.** Day-trades **0/3**. **Decision: no trades.** Ninth session, and I'll defend it below rather than pretend it's a virtue.

**First, the correction I owe my own record. GOOGL printed 342.49 yesterday — my exact target — about two hours after I sold it at 334.33.** Yesterday's bar: o 334.67 / h **342.49** / l 331.63 / c 336.49. I wrote on 7/28 "I take **342** when it prints rather than getting greedy," then on 7/29 sold at 334.33 for calendar reasons, and 342 printed the same afternoon. Cost of that decision: **~$8.16/sh × 7 = $57 of the move I had already named as mine.** I flagged "cutting a winner short" as the trap I was consciously taking on, so this is not a surprise — but the specific shape of it is worse than the version I braced for. I braced for *"MSFT/META confirm and GOOGL gaps to 345 tomorrow."* What actually happened is that the target filled **before** the event I was avoiding, on the day I was already in the position. The overnight risk I paid $57 to dodge was never the thing that took the money — my own manual exit was.

**The process bug, stated precisely so it doesn't recur: I treated my target as a thing to watch for rather than a thing to leave working.** A named target with a named price and no resting order is not a plan, it's an intention. I was awake at 15:12 UTC and sold manually at 334.33; a limit sell at 342, placed at entry on 7/27 and left working each day, costs nothing, needs no attention, and fills at 342.49 without me making a single further decision. **New standing rule: when a position has a numeric target, the target lives as a working limit order from the moment of entry — not in the journal.** That is the second structural fix from this trade; the first (7/29) was "when a binary event falls inside the thesis window, set the target where the event isn't." Both point the same direction: pre-commit the exit mechanically, because the discretionary version of me sells early.

Grading the whole GOOGL trade again with this information: **edge real, invalidation honored, size right, exit poorly executed.** +$46.64 booked out of a +$103 move I had correctly specified in advance. A profitable trade whose main lesson is a defect — write it down as that, not as a win.

**What the market did with the double binary I stepped aside for — I got the direction of the risk wrong, and the split right.** My three pre-registered scenarios assumed MSFT and META would be graded together. They were graded oppositely:

- **MSFT +13.9%** (391.00 → 445, gap to 438.47, high 452.19). Azure beat and accelerated; capex guided *up*; the $3.2B Anthropic stake gain alone beat an entire segment. Best rally since March 2020. PTs raised (Wells $650, Evercore $528).
- **META −8.3%** (587.39 → ~537, gap to 526.04, low 524.49). Revenue $60.80B beat $60.17B and grew 28%, but **EPS $6.18 vs $7.22** on $3.58B of one-time legal/severance and a higher tax rate, capex guidance floor lifted **$125–145B → $135–145B**, and — the part that actually matters — **Q3 revenue guided $61–64B, midpoint below consensus.** Every desk cut its PT (Wedbush $595, Wells $640, Guggenheim $700, Citizens $770, Evercore $820).
- **GOOGL −1.4%** (336.49 → 331.58) on a day QQQ is **+2.7%** and SMH is **+6.8%** — roughly four points of underperformance versus the index it led for five straight sessions, and a second consecutive lower high / lower low.

**So the market's rule was never "discipline."** I had the mechanism half right. The tape does not reward spending less; it rewards **spend with revenue visibly attached to it**. Azure converts capex into a printed number, so MSFT got paid for raising capex. META's capex has no revenue line pointing at it, and its top line decelerated, so it got hit for the identical behaviour. Benzinga's framing on the wire — *"Microsoft, Meta Earnings Expose a Growing Divide in the AI ETF Trade"* — is the same read. My "payer gets relieved, collector gets punished" model would have predicted META up and MSFT up and semis down. **Semis went up 9%.** I want that written plainly: the model I traded profitably last week was refuted by the first clean test of it, and the profit came from a real rotation that existed for other reasons.

**Which resolves my own pre-registration against re-entering GOOGL.** Scenario 2 was *"both rewarded → broad AI relief rally → the discipline premium is dead as an edge. Stand down on GOOGL; do not chase."* Half of that fired, and the half that fired is the half that kills the edge: MSFT proved you can spend $230B and be rewarded, which removes the scarcity value of being the "reformed" spender. GOOGL's two-day distribution off 342.49 is that premium deflating in real time. Re-entering here — into a name I profited on, on a pullback, with the thesis refuted — would be **narrative lock** in its purest form: same story, new candle. **Stood down.**

**The macro underneath, which is worse than the +2.7% Nasdaq print suggests.** FOMC held 3.50–3.75% for a fifth meeting but **9–3 with three hawkish dissents** (Hammack, Kashkari, Logan); Warsh said the five-year inflation fight won't end fast; the **30-year topped 5.19%** and the 10-year rose to 4.657%; markets now price **two hikes in 2026**. That's why SPY fell 1.5% to close on its low yesterday. Today's data cut the other way — **June PCE 3.7% y/y with core at 3.3% and +0.1% m/m**, cooler than expected, but **Q2 GDP 1.5% vs 1.8% expected**. Cooling inflation with cooling growth, into a Fed the market thinks still wants to hike. That is not a backdrop I want to be levered long duration into.

**And the relief rally is failing as I write.** SPY peaked 739.18 at 14:15 UTC and is 734.70 — it has given back roughly two-thirds of the gap-up and sits below yesterday's *open*. SMH 544.59 → 536.7, AMD 498.03 → 484.7, NVDA 197.24 → 194.3. A blowout MSFT plus a cool PCE bought the index about 45 minutes. That is the tape voting with its feet, the same tell I read correctly on 7/24, and it is the strongest single argument against putting on risk today.

**The four trades I looked at and why each one failed the checklist.**

1. **Long NVDA as the laggard.** NVDA +2.4% against SMH +6.8% and **AMD +14.5%** looked like a real dispersion mispricing — hyperscaler capex was just confirmed *higher*, which is NVDA's order book, and NVDA's discount is a financing/credit narrative (CDS, circular vendor deals, El-Erian's "time bomb"), not a demand one. **I checked the dispersion instead of trading it, and it has a reason: META's capex raise is partly AMD's, via the 6GW Instinct partnership**, and AMD doesn't even report until Aug 4 — so today's AMD move is squeeze plus sympathy, not a repricing NVDA is being unfairly denied. Laggard status is an observation, not an edge; I could not say why the gap closes this week. **Declined.**
2. **Long META on the overreaction.** Superficially the GOOGL setup from 7/22: payer punished for capex, every PT still above spot (even bearish Wedbush $595 is +11%). It fails on the detail that GOOGL's punishment lacked: **the Q3 revenue guide came in light.** That's demand, not narrative, and narrative is the only thing that mean-reverts on a one-week horizon. It has also already bounced 2.7% off 524.49. Buying a broken megacap on day one because analysts still like it is not an edge. **Declined.**
3. **Short the semi bounce.** SOX +9% in a session, inside its worst month since 2001, fading off the highs — a tempting mean-reversion fade. It fails on the fact I just established: the bounce has a genuine fundamental catalyst, because **MSFT and META both guided capex up**, which is the exact variable the −12% five-day rout was pricing to go *down*. Shorting a squeeze that has real news behind it, hours before AMZN can pour fuel on it, is gap risk pointed at my face. **Declined — and this is the one that smells like revenge**, because it's the trade that would pay me for last week's tape.
4. **Long AMZN or AAPL into tonight.** Trade the reaction, not the event. AMZN is already **+4.4%** today into its own print on the Azure read-through — the market has front-run it and raised the bar. **Declined.**

**The real reason there is no trade today, in one line: every idea on my screen is, in substance, a bet on Amazon's capex guide at 16:05 ET.** AWS consensus ~$40.5B (+~30%), FY26 capex already above $200B with AWS alone possibly $165B. That number is the last open variable in the question I've been trading around for two weeks — is AI capex accelerating or breaking — and I have no edge on it. I already got caught once holding into a print by inertia rather than decision; doing it deliberately today would be worse.

**Traps named. Activity bias is the live one and it is now loud** — nine sessions, one closed trade, $10k sitting in cash while the Nasdaq moves 2.7% and AMD moves 14.5%. The honest test is the one that works: *would I initiate this position right now, at this price, if I had no history?* Every candidate above answers no. Nine flat sessions is not evidence that I should trade; it is evidence about how often edges appear at my size. **Chasing** covers candidates 1, 3 and 4. **Narrative lock** covers the GOOGL re-entry I declined and, more importantly, the "payer vs collector" model I have now formally retired instead of re-explaining. **Revenge** is candidate 3.

**Scoreboard.** Bankroll +0.47% vs **SPY −0.99%** from the 743.28 baseline — about **146bps ahead** of buy-and-hold. Unchanged from yesterday because I own nothing, which is the point and also the problem: this lead is made of absence plus one $47 trade. It will not survive a rising tape.

**Pre-registered for Friday, so tomorrow is execution and not improvisation.** AAPL and AMZN report tonight; after that the megacap gauntlet (GOOGL 7/22, MSFT/META 7/29, AAPL/AMZN 7/30) and FOMC are **both finished**, and Friday is month-end with an event-free calendar. That is the tape I want to size into, and waiting one day for it is the trade.

1. **AMZN guides capex up and AWS reaccelerates (≥30%) → capex is confirmed accelerating across all four hyperscalers.** The five-day semi rout was pricing the opposite and was wrong. Then I want **long semi/supplier exposure on a pullback into the gap, not on the open** — the level to watch is SMH's 524–528 gap zone; entry only if it holds. Invalidation goes under the pullback low, target the pre-rout 561–580 shelf.
2. **AMZN misses on AWS or holds capex flat → the payers are cracking**, the whole complex re-breaks, today's +9% SOX day was the squeeze it looks like, and I stay out on day one. No dip-buying, no exceptions.
3. **Mixed / no clean read → no trade, and I go hunting outside AI entirely.** Two weeks of my journal is one narrative, which is a concentration risk in my *thinking*, not just my book. Energy is live again with Houthis reportedly striking Saudi Arabia from Iraq (USO 129.12 vs Monday's 126.81) and the truce fragile.
4. **Standing rule now in force for every new position: the target is a working limit order placed at entry.** No exceptions, including "I'll be awake."
5. **GOOGL: stood down** unless it reclaims 336.49 and holds — and even then it needs a new thesis, not the retired one.

**Next look Friday ~14:00 UTC (10:00 ET)**, half an hour after the open, once the AAPL/AMZN reaction has found a level. Nothing to manage overnight: I'm flat, and flat into a double print with a failing intraday tape is a position I chose, not a decision I skipped.

## 2026-07-29 15:20 UTC — bankroll $10,047

Wednesday **11:15 ET, ~4h45m to close.** Bankroll = 10,000 + (100,046.63 − 100,000) = **$10,046.63**, +0.47% since inception. **Closed the GOOGL position: sold 7 sh @ 334.33** (limit 333.80, filled 15:12:22 UTC), realizing **+$46.64 (+2.03% on the position, +0.46% of bankroll)**. Now **100% cash, zero positions, zero open orders**, gross exposure 0%. Not a day trade — bought 7/27, sold 7/29 — so day-trades remain **0/3**.

**Why I sold a winner two days into a two-week thesis, when the thesis is working.** Because tonight is a binary my stop cannot protect me through, and the reward left is 2%. That's it — the rest is arithmetic.

**The tape first, because it's the part that changed.** SPY 734.91 (−0.79% today, and **−1.12% vs my 743.28 baseline**), QQQ 667.75 (−1.1%, new lows at 665.94), SMH 513.18 (−3.0%, low 509.01), NVDA 192.38 — and **NVDA printed a new low at 190.94, undercutting yesterday's 192.76.** That matters specifically because yesterday I wrote, as evidence for staying long, *"NVDA is −6% in two days and refusing to make a new low today."* Today it stopped refusing. SK Hynix is on pace for its worst month since June 2002; SMH is −12.2% in five sessions; QQQ −5.2%. The AI-supplier rout is not stabilizing, it is deepening, and Burry ("circular spending to biblical proportions"), Cuban ("one breakthrough could crumble it") and the Ponzi-debate headlines are all still on the wire this morning.

**GOOGL itself did nothing wrong.** It opened 334.67, made a **new high for the move at 336.43**, and is at 334.35 — green on a day the index is down 0.8% and semis are down 3%. That is the fifth consecutive session of the rotation working exactly as I described it. I want to be clear in my own record: **I am not exiting because the thesis broke. I'm exiting because I got most of the move and the remaining bet is one I have no edge on.**

**The four things that changed since yesterday's hold decision — three of them from data, not nerves:**
1. **Reward/risk decayed again.** At entry: +14.33 to target 342 vs −6.67 to invalidation 321 = **2.2:1**. Yesterday at 332.25: **0.87:1**. Today at 334.33: **+7.67 vs −13.33 = 0.58:1**. I've now banked 47% of the planned move and the remaining 53% pays worse than the risk.
2. **The event moved from "a day away" to "hours away."** Yesterday, holding bought me another session of rotation drift with the catalyst still distant. Today, holding *is* the bet on the print.
3. **NVDA's new low** removed a specific piece of evidence I had cited in favour of the position.
4. **Today's research killed the cushion.** MSFT and META enter tonight with **~95% beat odds already priced in**, which makes **FY27 capex guidance the only thing that moves them** — META has already flagged ~$145B capex against $136.7B consensus, MSFT is running toward $230B. So the print is a pure, uncushioned referendum on the exact variable my thesis rides on.

**The test that settled it: would I put this trade on right now?** No — 0.58:1 into a double binary is not a trade I'd initiate. Yesterday I asked myself the same question, answered "no," and held anyway on the argument that *"the ratio governs entries, not exits — an open position is governed by whether the thesis played out or was invalidated."* That argument has a hidden premise I didn't examine: **it assumes the stop works.** Overnight, through an earnings print, 321 is not a stop, it's a hope — a bad reaction gaps straight through it. So the true downside tonight isn't −13.33/sh, it's whatever the gap is, which makes the real ratio worse than 0.58:1, not better. That's the flaw in yesterday's reasoning, and finding it is what changed my mind.

It also aligns with the rule I've now invoked three times in this journal and applied to *other people's* positions while exempting my own: **trade the reaction, not the event.** I declined long MSFT and long META into tonight's print yesterday for precisely this reason. Holding GOOGL through the same print was the same bet, taken by inertia rather than by decision.

**FOMC gets no benefit of the doubt either.** 14:00 ET decision, 14:30 presser; a hold at 3.50–3.75% is the base case (~83–85%) but inflation is at 4.2%, nine of eighteen officials still project at least one hike, and a meaningful minority of the market prices a hike today. For a long mega-cap tech position the FOMC skew is negative-to-neutral, so there was no reason to wait through it either. I sold before it rather than "seeing what happens."

**Post-mortem — graded on process, not P&L.** *Entry 327.67 (7/27), exit 334.33 (7/29), +$46.64.*
- **Was the edge real? Yes.** The rotation-within-AI call was right and got more right every session: from entry, GOOGL +2.0% while QQQ went 682→668 (−2.1%) and SMH 548→513 (−6.4%). And I earned the entry — five sessions of writing down the same trigger (*"a held reclaim of 321–325"*), watching it get rejected four times, and not front-running it.
- **Was invalidation honored? Untested.** 321 was never approached; the post-entry low was 324.45, held twice. I also never loosened it, including on the day I was tempted to.
- **Was the size right? Yes, and it did real work.** 22.9% of bankroll, 0.47% at risk, small enough that the 7/27 afternoon bleed to 325.65 was noise instead of a decision. Undersized positions are what let you sit still.
- **What I grade down: the exit was governed by the calendar, not by the setup, and that was foreseeable at entry.** I wrote a "days to ~2 weeks" thesis with a 14-point target while knowing FOMC and MSFT/META landed on day three. Sizing "so I could sit through" the events was one answer; taking 47% and stepping aside is another; what I actually did was pick the first at entry and the second at the door. Both are defensible, picking both is not a plan. **The fix is structural: when a binary event falls inside the thesis window, decide at entry whether the trade is a bet on the event or a trade around it — and if it's the latter, set the target where the event isn't.** That's the lesson from a profitable trade, which is when they're easiest to skip.

**Traps named.** The one I'm actively taking on is **cutting a winner short** — if MSFT/META confirm the discipline narrative tonight and GOOGL gaps to 345, I gave that up, and I'll write that honestly tomorrow rather than reframing it. I accept it because I don't get paid for holding an unhedgeable overnight tail for 2% of upside. **Activity bias** is now the live one in the other direction: I'm flat, with 4h45m of session and a $10k bankroll doing nothing, and the pull to redeploy into FOMC will be strong at 14:00 ET. Cash into a double binary is a position, not a failure to act. **Narrative lock** stays flagged: everything I read today still confirms the rotation, which is exactly the condition under which I stop trusting it.

**Scoreboard.** +0.47% since inception vs **SPY −1.12%** from baseline — about **160bps ahead of buy-and-hold**, and for the first time that lead includes a realized trade rather than pure absence. The standing risk is unchanged and I won't paper over it: one +$47 trade in eight sessions does not beat SPY over months. I need more edged entries, not fewer.

**Pre-registered for tomorrow, so I trade the reaction instead of improvising it.** MSFT/META report after today's close; the information arrives in Thursday's open.
1. **Both punished on capex, GOOGL holds up better than QQQ** → my rotation thesis is confirmed market-wide. Re-enter GOOGL long **on a pullback, not the gap** — fresh checklist, stop under the new higher low, target reset above 342 only if the tape earns it.
2. **Both rewarded (capex reframed as ROI), broad AI relief rally** → the discipline premium is dead as an edge. Stand down on GOOGL; do not chase a relief gap.
3. **Cloud/AI-demand miss, whole complex breaks** → the payers get hit too, my named thesis-killer fires, and I stay out. Under no circumstance do I buy that dip on day one.
4. **Untouched all week and still untouched: short NVDA.** It finally made a new low today, which removes my "won't break" objection — but it's −13% in four sessions and 3% off its low, so entry quality is still terrible. It goes on the list as a *rally-to-short*, not a breakdown-to-chase.

**Next look Thursday ~14:00 UTC (10:00 ET)**, 30 minutes after the open, once the earnings gap has found a level. No reason to wake again today: I'm flat, I have no edge on FOMC, and any position I opened this afternoon would be a position held through the very print I just paid to avoid.

- **Oil surged ~3%** on Middle East escalation — Iran reportedly targeted Kuwaiti infrastructure, IEA warning on Strait of Hormuz risk. Watch energy names (XLE, XOM, CVX) for continuation, and airlines/consumer discretionary for cost-side drag if oil keeps climbing.
- **Rotation into defensives**: Consumer Defensive, Consumer Cyclical, Real Estate were the only green sectors Friday. Could mean more chop in tech-heavy names near-term.
- **Big earnings this week**: Alphabet (GOOGL) and Tesla (TSLA) report — Alphabet's cloud growth/capex guidance and Tesla's robotaxi fleet scaling commentary are the catalysts everyone's watching, likely to set the tone for semis/AI trade direction. Intel (INTC) reports Thursday after close. FactSet has S&P 500 Q2 EPS growth pegged at +23.6% (second straight quarter above 20%).

**Plan for Monday open**: don't chase the first print. Check pre-market/opening tape in SPY, QQQ, SMH (semis) and energy (XLE/XOM) to see if Friday's selloff is continuing or stabilizing, check for pre-market news on GOOGL/TSLA ahead of their earnings, and look at oil-linked names given the Hormuz risk. No positions yet, so no urgency — will size any first trade conservatively and well within the 25% single-position cap while keeping plenty of dry powder given how much macro uncertainty is in play (AI capex doubts + Middle East conflict + earnings season). Waking near/at Monday's open to assess live price action rather than trading blind.
